21 
Plant in Service Model 
22 
Q. 
What is the net plant model? 
23 


--- Page 24 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
23 
 
A. 
The net plant model is a tool used to forecast 
1 
projected plant and depreciation reserve 
2 
balances.  The plant model in this case begins 
3 
with the most recent actual gross plant as of 
4 
December 31, 2021, and adds and removes 
5 
forecasted additions and retirements, 
6 
respectively, to forecast the gross plant on a 
7 
month-to-month basis.  Additionally, using the 
8 
depreciation rates supplied by the Staff 
9 
Depreciation Panel, the net plant model can 
10 
forecast the depreciation expense and 
11 
depreciation reserve.  The net plant is the 
12 
gross plant minus the depreciation reserve.  
13 
Q. 
How did the Panel develop the forecast for 
14 
electric net plant and depreciation expense? 
15 
A. 
We used Company workpapers to develop and adjust 
16 
our forecasted plant additions and retirements, 
17 
resulting in our recommended net plant, 
18 
depreciation reserve and depreciation expense 
19 
levels.  The Rate Year depreciation rates used 
20 
in our model were supplied by the Staff 
21 
Depreciation Panel.  
22

Q. 
Please describe the Company’s Queensboro Bridge 
16 
Risk Mitigation Project. 
17 
A. 
On page 58 of the EIOP’s initial testimony, the 
18 
Company states that there are six 138 kV feeders 
19 
and six 69 kV feeders that traverse the 
20 
Queensboro Bridge.  All the 138 kV feeders going 
21 
from Vernon Substation to E40th Street Nos 1 and 
22 
2 and Murray Hill Substations traverse the 
23 


--- Page 30 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
29 
 
Queensboro Bridge; therefore, a failure of the 
1 
bridge would be an event common to all the 
2 
circuits simultaneously and could result in 
3 
significant feeder failures and electric 
4 
customer outages.  This project would remove the 
5 
six 138 kV feeders and replace them with new 
6 
cable in trenchless crossings underneath the 
7 
East River.  The 138 kV feeders would be 
8 
prioritized for replacement under this project 
9 
and the design would consider the need to 
10 
eventually remove the remaining six 69 kV 
11 
feeders off the Queensboro Bridge. 
12 
Q. 
What funding level did the Company request for 
13 
this program during RY1, RY2 and RY3? 
14 
A. 
The Company is requesting capital investments of 
15 
approximately $20.0 million, $80.0 million and 
16 
$80.0 million in RY1, RY2, and RY3, 
17 
respectively, for this project. 
18 
Q. 
What is the Company’s justification for this 
19 
project? 
20 
A. 
On Page 58 of its initial testimony, the EIOP 
21 
states that the Queensboro bridge was identified 
22 
by New York City Department of Transportation, 
23 


--- Page 31 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
30 
 
or NYCDOT, as a common mode of failure and as 
1 
the number one risk in high impact, low 
2 
probability.  If the bridge fails, it would take 
3 
out most of the electricity supply to the east 
4 
side of Manhattan.  There have been multiple 
5 
electrical joint failures on these feeders due 
6 
to cable movement in the last few years.  Every 
7 
time there is an electrical feeder or joint 
8 
failure, it requires extensive repairs that 
9 
necessitate coordination with NYCDOT and 
10 
construction of scaffolding to be able to reach 
11 
and work on the electric feeders.  To replace an 
12 
electric joint, the whole feeder pipe must be 
13 
lowered towards the pier and, due to repeated 
14 
repairs, the pipe is getting close enough to the 
15 
pier where it cannot be lowered any further.  
16 
The only way to mitigate these risks is to 
17 
remove the feeders from the bridge and install 
18 
them in trenchless crossings underneath the East 
19 
River.  Moreover, there have been multiple joint 
20 
failures on these feeders due to cable movement 
21 
in the last few years.  In 2015, Con Edison 
22 
spent $56 million to replace 11 of these joints; 
23 


--- Page 32 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
31 
 
however, additional joint issues have continued 
1 
and, in 2019, there was a failure on feeder 
2 
38M01. 
3 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the Company’s 
4 
approach? 
5 
A. 
Yes, partially.  We agree with the scope of this 
6 
project, as well as with the cost estimates, but 
7 
disagree with the timing.  In its response to IR 
8 
DPS-5-302, included in Exhibit__(SEIOP-1), the 
9 
Company states that this project will take eight 
10 
years to complete, which is a long lead time to 
11 
resolve such an important issue.  If multiple 
12 
failures were to occur during these eight years, 
13 
load shedding and large-scale customer outages 
14 
could take place on the east side of Manhattan.  
15 
We recommend that the Company accelerate 
16 
completion of this project from eight to five 
17 
years.

19 
38R51 and 38R52 Replacement Project.

A. 
On page 82 of Exhibit__(EIOP-3), Schedule 3, Con 
21 
Edison proposes to construct two new 138 kV

23 


--- Page 33 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
32 
 
transmission feeders to replace two existing 138 
1 
kV Medium Pressure Fluid Filled, or MPFF, 
2

3 
Station to the Wainwright Station on Staten 
4 
Island.  The Company proposes to replace the 
5

6

7

8

9

10 
Edison through a combination of open-cut 
11

Q. 
What funding level did the Company request for 
14 
this project during RY1, RY2 and RY3? 
15 
A. 
According to Exhibit__(EIOP-3 UPD), the total 
16

17

18 
million in RY1. 
19 
Q. 
What is the Company’s justification for this 
20 
project? 
21 
A. 
On page 82 of its Exhibit__(EIOP-3), Con Edison 
22 
states that the routing of feeders 38R51 and 
23 


--- Page 34 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
33 
 
38R52 addresses the risks of dielectric fluid 
1 
leaks in environmentally sensitive areas 
2 
(wetlands and the Fresh Kills Creek) where 
3 
repair access may be very difficult.  In 2017, 
4 
38R52 developed a leak in the Fresh Kills Creek 
5 
section of the feeder.  This event resulted in 
6 
the loss of over 1,600 gallons of dielectric 
7 
fluid to the waterway and required multiple 
8 
extended outages to make permanent repairs.  One 
9 
of the outages needed to make temporary repairs 
10 
occurred during a high load period and required 
11 
the deployment of mobile generation for 
12 
contingency planning.  The Company claims that 
13 
the replacement of 38R51 and 38R52 with XLPE 
14 
cable in duct bank would significantly lower 
15 
current environmental and reliability risks.  
16 
Additionally, the Company claims that the use of 
17 
an XLPE cable system would eliminate 
18 
approximately 300-400 hours of maintenance, 
19 
reduce unplanned outages, improve environmental 
20 
performance, and help to standardize labor 
21 
expertise and spare inventory.  The Company 
22 
states that maintaining and repairing feeders 
23 


--- Page 35 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
34 
 
38R51 and 38R52 require a specialized workforce 
1 
and non-standard spare inventory.  In addition, 
2 
the Company notes that spare inventory must be 
3 
carefully managed as the original equipment 
4 
manufacturer, OEM, no longer makes the cable 
5 
used to construct 38R51 and 38R52.  A manhole, 
6 
as a leak location for 38R51 or 38R52, poses 
7 
another unique reliability risk.  Per 
8 
Occupational Safety and Health Administration 
9 
regulations, a structure housing an MPFF circuit 
10 
found to be leaking (considered a D fault 
11 
condition) cannot be re-entered until such 
12 
circuit is de-energized.  Feeders 38R51 and 
13 
38R52 share the same route and have common 
14 
manholes.  Due to this configuration, the 
15 
possibility exists that both circuits could have 
16 
a leak in the same manhole at the same time. 
17 
Q. 
Does the Panel recommend any adjustments for 
18 
this project during the Rate Year?

A. 
Yes.  The Panel recommends eliminating the 
20 
Feeder 38R51 and 38R52 Replacement Project.

Q. 
Describe the Panel’s justification for its 
22

23 


--- Page 36 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
35 
 
A. 
We recommend eliminating this project for 
1 
several reasons.  First, the Company has not 
2 
demonstrated an immediate need for the Feeder 
3 
38R51 and 38R52 Replacement Project.  The 
4 
Company has not shown that, without this 
5 
project, there is an imminent threat to the 
6 
reliable operation of the Company’s system.  
7 
Second, the new feeder capacity rating would be 
8 
limited by the substation’s transformer rating.  
9 
Third, the Panel does not agree with the 
10 
Company’s argument regarding scarcity and long-
11 
lead times for feeders 38R51 and 38R52 repair 
12 
and replacement parts.  Attachment 5 to Con 
13 
Edison’s response to IR DPS-5-292 included in 
14 
Exhibit__(SEIOP-1), shows that most leak repairs 
15 
performed on feeders 38R51 and 38R52 required a 
16 
lead re-wipe.  In response to IR DPS-21-604, 
17 
included in Exhibit__(SEIOP-1), the Company 
18 
explains that a lead re-wipe repair involves de-
19 
energizing the feeder, uncovering and isolating 
20 
the location of the leak, removing the existing 
21 
lead wipe/sleeve and installing a new lead 
22 
wipe/sleeve.  In that same response, the Company 
23 


--- Page 37 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
36 
 
states that procurement of long lead materials 
1 
and equipment is not necessary for re-wipe 
2 
repairs; if replacement cable is needed for 
3 
feeders 38R51 and 38R52, the Company could 
4 
acquire this material in nine months.  However, 
5 
this type of replacement has rarely been 
6 
necessary over the life of feeders 38R51 and 
7 
38R52.  Finally, the Company argues that feeders 
8 
38R51 and 38R52 pose an environmental risk to 
9 
the Fresh Kills Creek.  However, in 2017, the 
10 
Company replaced both submarine cable sections 
11 
of feeders 38R51 and 38R52 under the Fresh Kills 
12 
Creek after 38R52 developed a leak.  Therefore, 
13 
the risk of additional dielectric fluid leaks on 
14 
these new portions of the feeders has been 
15 
greatly reduced.  According to Attachment 5 of 
16 
its response to DPS-5-292, the repair time on 
17 
those feeders was two to five days and did not 
18 
require the Company to deploy any mobile 
19 
generators.  The Company also noted in that 
20 
response that there were no leaks on those 
21 
feeders in 2021.

Q.

19

20 
A.

21 
the Company states that, as a transformer ages, 
22 
more corrective maintenance is required and the 
23 


--- Page 54 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
53 
 
risk of in-service failure increases.  In-
1 
service failures impact reliability because they 
2 
can occur at high load periods and/or be 
3 
coincident with other outages.  Degradation of 
4 
the insulation is directly dependent on the 
5 
duration of time that transformers are operated 
6 
at higher temperatures.  Con Edison’s climate 
7 
change projections include longer and higher 
8 
intensity heat waves.  Additionally, 
9

10 
anticipated to significantly increase in the 
11 
latter part of this decade and that shift will 
12 
mean that transformers will be more heavily 
13 
loaded during the winter months, a period of the 
14 
year that they were not previously.  Given fleet 
15 
demographics, the lead time in replacing a 
16 
failed transformer, the risks associated with 
17 
in-service failures, and the anticipated 
18 
increase in heat waves and EOH, the Company 
19 
maintains that it must increase its planned 
20 
replacement of transformers from three units to 
21 
eight units per year.

Q. 
Please explain the Relay Modification Program.  
7 
A. 
This program replaces relay protection systems 
8 
at area and transmission substations.  This 
9 
program will continue to target transmission 
10 
relays that exhibit reliability issues, such as 
11 
HCB/LCB relays, legacy microprocessor systems 
12 
and multicomponent semi-conductors systems.  HCB 
13 
is a protection relay designed for the complete 
14 
phase and ground protection of two and three 
15 
terminal transmission lines and LCB is a 
16 
communication relay that transmits information 
17 
between two terminals.  This program will be 
18 
expanded to include upgrades to area station bus 
19 
and feeder protection, installations that 
20 
eliminate single points of failure (TPL-001-5 
21 
Upgrades) and replacement for some early 
22 
microprocessor relays, including Under Frequency 
23 


--- Page 66 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
65 
 
Load Shedding, or UFLS, panels. 
1 
Q. 
What is the Company’s justification for this 
2 
program? 
3 
A. 
On page 174 of Exhibit__(EIOP-3), Schedule 3, 
4 
the Company states that relay systems are 
5 
critical in the protection of electric 
6 
transmission and distribution systems.  The 
7 
reliable and timely operation of a relay system 
8 
electrically isolates a faulted piece of 
9 
equipment and protects the rest of the 
10 
transmission and distribution system from any 
11 
conditions that may cause further damage.  The 
12 
Company states that a more standardized approach 
13 
to upgrades, however, and an expansion of the 
14 
program to include area station relays, UFLS 
15 
panel upgrades and Transmission Planning 
16 
projects is critical.  The standardized approach 
17 
will help the Company adapt to changing climate 
18 
conditions by improving restoration times 
19 
following extreme weather events.  Modernizing 
20 
relay systems by moving away from legacy 
21 
electromechanical systems towards 
22 
microprocessor-based systems will facilitate 
23 


--- Page 67 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
66 
 
real time data collection on the health of the 
1 
transmission and substation system and the 
2 
ability to remotely recover from events.  The 
3 
Company states that increased replacements of 
4 
area substation relay systems that are either 
5 
self-resetting or can be reset remotely is 
6 
essential to preventing events that may impact 
7 
many customers simultaneously, as well as 
8 
improving the time needed to recover from these 
9 
events and others triggered by extreme weather.  
10 
Additionally, the newer microprocessors offer 
11 
increased intelligence and integrate advanced 
12 
technology to provide security against common 
13 
industry wide issues such as telecommunication 
14 
circuit performance issues leading to mis-
15 
operations. 
16

16

17

18

19

interrupter age reduction factor.  According to 
1

2

3

4

5

6

7

8 
Q. 
What is the justification provided by the 
9 
Company? 
10 
A. 
Circuit switchers function to provide electrical 
11 
isolation to substation equipment or 
12 
transmission lines during planned outages and/or 
13 
fault conditions.  If a circuit switcher does 
14 
not operate as intended, more equipment will 
15 
need to be isolated than would otherwise be 
16 
required, referred to as overtripping.  
17 
Overtripping can lead to contingencies that may 
18 
require load shedding.  The Company will target 
19 
some circuit switchers for replacement because 
20 
they are leaking SF6 gas, which is a greenhouse 
21 
gas.  Replacing circuit switchers that are in 
22 
poor health will help maintain reliability and 
23 


--- Page 115 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
114 
 
help the with climate change mitigation efforts.  
1 
Q. 
What is Con Edison’s funding request? 
2 
A. 
The Company has requested $1.4 million per year 
3 
for RY1, RY2 and RY3. 
4 
Q. 
What is the Panel’s recommendation? 
5

6 
isolation of substation equipment, we support 
7 
the program.  When comparing the Company’s 
8

9

Q. 
Please explain the Relay Protection 
16 
Communication Upgrade Program. 
17 
A. 
According to page 180 of Exhibit__(EIOP-3), 
18 
Schedule 3, the intent of this program is to 
19 
remove from service inadequate communications 
20 
infrastructure used, in part or in whole, for 
21 
relay protection and replace it with modern, 
22 
actively supported, and reliable communications 
23 


--- Page 122 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
121 
 
infrastructure.  For most locations, this 
1 
program will also provide two independent 
2 
communication systems for relay protection, 
3 
which will increase the reliability of the 
4 
electric system by eliminating single point mode 
5 
of failure in the relay protection communication 
6 
networks.  The work is to take place at various 
7 
locations throughout the system.  The work is 
8 
divided into three categories: 1) the upgrade of 
9 
Corporate Communication Telephone Network, or 
10 
CCTN upgrades, 2) the upgrade of Verizon 
11 
communications infrastructure, or Verizon 
12 
upgrades, and 3) the upgrade of relay protection 
13 
equipment or relay communication upgrades. 
14 
Q. 
What is the justification provided by the 
15 
Company for this project?  
16 
A. 
According to page 181 of Exhibit__(EIOP-3), 
17 
Schedule 3, the underlying reasons for Con 
18 
Edison’s proposed upgrades are multifaceted, 
19 
with each aspect adding to the overall goal of 
20 
increasing system reliability and resiliency.  
21 
The Company’s primary concern is the migration 
22 
of relay protection systems off failed, failing, 
23 


--- Page 123 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
122 
 
or problematic communication links.  Since the 
1 
repair of these circuits is not under the 
2 
authority of Con Edison, it is difficult to 
3 
control the timeframe in which the equipment is 
4 
returned to service as the Company relies on the 
5 
service provider (usually Verizon) to repair the 
6 
copper communication line.  The secondary 
7 
concern is the mitigation of single mode point 
8 
of failure situations that may exist in the 
9 
communication networks that serve independent 
10 
relay protection systems protecting the same 
11 
power system elements.  The reason for this is 
12 
to prevent a single mode point of failure in the 
13 
relay protection system in which the loss of a 
14 
single element of the protection system (which 
15 
includes its communication elements) would cause 
16 
both independent relay protection systems 
17 
protecting a single power system element to be 
18 
defeated simultaneously.  If both relay 
19 
protection systems protecting a single element 
20 
were to rely on a single fiber network only, be 
21 
it Verizon or CCTN, and that network were to be 
22 
compromised, then with a single failure, the 
23 


--- Page 124 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
123 
 
power system element would be unprotected. 
1 
Furthermore, if there is only one communication 
2 
system servicing a station, and that 
3 
communication system was to be compromised, then 
4 
the entire station could lose protection for 
5 
multiple elements simultaneously.  Con Edison 
6 
maintains that such a situation must be avoided 
7 
as it has the potential to leave a large, 
8 
contiguous portion of the system at risk of 
9 
outage if a failure were to occur.  Two 
10 
independent communication networks are necessary 
11 
to mitigate this, which Con Edison proposes to 
12 
implement using CCTN and Verizon fiber services. 
13

17

18

19 
it is the average of six years of data.  This 
20

21

22

23 


--- Page 144 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP

points to Dominion’s and Florida Power and 
1

4 
different than the program described by Con 
5 
Edison.  For example, FPL began its pilot

12 
Edison did not describe critical facilities as a 
13 
consideration when selecting areas for

undergrounding.

worse performing circuit list and feeders with

high tree System Average Interruption Frequency

10

15

18

19

20

21 
diversity.  In addition, due to supply chain 
22 
issues, the cost of an SOS sensor rose from 
23 


--- Page 163 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
162 
 
$1,500 in 2017 to $5,000 in 2020.  While sensor 
1 
prices have increased, the technology has not 
2 
developed to a state where the sensors can 
3 
reliably provide early warning of an impending 
4 
manhole event.  Also, SOS sensors are designed 
5 
to detect hot spots, or defective cable and 
6 
splices, via infra-red imaging.  In 2020 and 
7 
2021, the Company identified only 32 conditions 
8 
and is unable to conclude which hot spot repair 
9 
prevented an event or the type of event that may 
10 
have been prevented.  Therefore, Con Edison’s 
11 
claim that SOS installations provide cost-saving 
12 
opportunities is unsupported and overstated.

implementation, the Company stated in its 
1

2 
years for significant results to be achieved and 
3 
that it is difficult for the Company to perform 
4

5 
Q. 
Are there any alternatives to replacing DBC with 
6 
cable in conduit?  
7 
A. 
Yes.  Cable rejuvenation is a proven technology 
8 
that has been used in the electric industry for 
9 
over 30 years, and it can be capitalized, which 
10 
means no impact to O&M budgets.  Cable 
11 
rejuvenation allows utilities to fix and extend 
12 
the life of damaged DBC and effectively add the 
13 
same value as installing a new cable section but 
14 
at a lower cost, with a shorter installation 
15 
time, and little to no customer and 
16 
environmental impact.  As stated in its response 
17 
to DPS-13-433, Con Edison plans to perform a 
18 
pilot program in 2022 to test the effectiveness 
19 
of cable rejuvenation on URD systems in 
20 
Westchester and Staten Island.  Recognizing that 
21 
the pilot program has not been completed, Con 
22 
Edison estimated that cable rejuvenation will 
23 


--- Page 204 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
203 
 
cost approximately $45 per foot of DBC but 
1 
cautioned that it may be difficult to 
2 
successfully rejuvenate cables in certain areas, 
3 
notably those with a high volume of cable 
4 
splices.     
5 
Q. 
What does the Panel recommend for the Targeted 
6 
DBC Program? 
7 
A. 
We recommend that the Company implement more 
8 
cable rejuvenation measures and less cable in 
9 
conduit installations because we expect the 
10 
implementation of the Company’s pilot program to 
11 
be successful since cable rejuvenation is a 
12 
proven measure that has been adopted in the 
13 
electric industry.  Moreover, it is more cost 
14 
effective than installing new cable in conduit, 
15 
costing $45 per foot of cable compared to 
16 
approximately $476 per foot to replace DBC with 
17 
new cable in conduit.  Because cable 
18 
rejuvenation is more cost-effective, Con Edison 
19 
will be able to address more cable sections with 
20 
the funding available for the Targeted DBC 
21 
Program, and this will have a bigger impact on 
22 
the reliability of URD systems in Con Edison’s 
23 


--- Page 205 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
204 
 
service territory.  For areas where cable 
1 
rejuvenation cannot be performed because of high 
2 
density of splices, Con Edison could use a 
3 
portion of the funding to perform DBC 
4 
replacement.  Additionally, Con Edison could use 
5 
all the program funding to replace DBC with 
6 
cable in conduit if the pilot proves to be 
7 
unsuccessful, but the Company should continue to 
8 
research new cable technologies or innovative 
9 
solutions that will help maintain or improve the 
10 
reliability of the Company’s URD systems.  
11

yearly budget that we are recommending will 
23

Q.

10

A.

12

13 
regional split the Company used for each 
14 
individual project.  Second, we multiplied the 
15 
increased capital investment in each region by 
16 
the region’s labor as a percentage of capital 
17 
that the Company derived to determine how much 
18 
of each regional capital investment is labor 
19 
driven.  Third, we divided those amounts by the 
20 
region’s estimated labor rate, which the Company 
21 
derived, to determine the incremental hours 
22 
needed in each region for the electric 
23 


--- Page 233 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
232 
 
distribution projects.  It should be noted that 
1

2 
of capital and labor rates.  For example, the 
3 
Bronx/Westchester region has a labor rate of 
4 
$189.27 per hour and a labor percentage of 
5 
24.10%, while the Brooklyn/Queens region has a 
6 
labor rate of $148.65 per hour and a labor 
7 
percentage of 27.42%.

employee, with a 20 percent overtime rate

included.  This resulted in an incremental need

of nine, 42, and 26 GUWs in the

Manhattan regions, respectively, and a decrease 
17 
of three GUWS in the Staten Island region.  This 
18 
results in a combined increase of 74 GUWs.

3 
Reliability Performance Mechanism 
4 
Q. 
Please provide a brief overview of the current 
5 
RPM for Con Edison. 
6 
A. 
The current RPM consists of four categories: 
7 
system-wide threshold standards; major outage; 
8 
remote monitoring system, or RMS; and program 
9 
standards.  The system-wide threshold standards, 
10 
major outage, and program standards categories 
11 
are comprised of multiple metrics.  Each metric 
12 
is used to monitor the Company’s performance in 
13 
a calendar year and a more detailed and complete 
14 
description of each is found in 
15 
Exhibit___(SEIOP-4).  The total negative revenue 
16 
adjustment in the current RPM is $197.5 million 
17 
for 2020 and 2021, and $200.5 million for 2022.  
18 
Q. 
What metrics are included in the existing 
19 
system-wide threshold standard category? 
20 
A. 
The system-wide threshold standard category 
21 
consists of the following metrics: radial SAIFI 
22 
with a threshold of 0.495; radial CAIDI, with a 
23 


--- Page 244 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
243 
 
threshold of 2.04 hours; network SAIFI with a 
1 
threshold of 0.0176; and network CAIDI with a 
2 
threshold of 6.89 hours. 
3 
Q. 
Did Con Edison propose any changes to the RPM? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  Con Edison proposed two changes to the 
5 
system-wide threshold standard category and one 
6 
change to the program standard category.  These 
7 
changes are discussed on pages 19 to 23 of the 
8 
Electric Storm Response and Resilience Panel’s 
9 
initial testimony and on pages 158 to 163 of the 
10 
EIOP’s initial testimony. 
11 
Q. 
Describe the first of the Company’s proposed 
12 
changes to the system-wide threshold standards. 
13 
A. 
As outlined on page 20 of the Electric Storm 
14 
Response and Resilience Panel’s initial 
15 
testimony, Con Edison is proposing an exclusion 
16 
to both the radial CAIDI and SAIFI metrics for 
17 
storms where the Company pre-mobilizes more than 
18 
100 contractor or utility mutual aid storm 
19 
restoration workers, even if the storm 
20 
ultimately does not constitute a major storm.   
21 
Q. 
What constitutes a major storm? 
22 
A. 
As defined in 16 NYCRR Part 97, a major storm is 
23 


--- Page 245 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
244 
 
one where at least 10 percent of the customers 
1 
within an operating area are interrupted or 
2 
customers are out of service for at least 24 
3 
hours.  All the State’s investor-owned utilities 
4 
currently have a major storm exclusion.   
5 
Q. 
For what reason is Con Edison proposing an 
6 
exclusion for storms where the Company pre-
7 
mobilizes more than 100 contractor or utility 
8 
mutual aid storm restoration workers? 
9 
A. 
Con Edison explains, on pages 20 and 21 of the 
10 
Electric Storm Response and Resilience Panel’s 
11 
initial testimony, that with the Company’s 
12 
current increased mobilization efforts, outages 
13 
resulting from storms that would have caused 
14 
interruption durations to meet the 24-hour 
15 
definition for a major storm, are now being 
16 
restored quicker.  The Company believes that 
17 
these interruption durations, although shorter 
18 
than the 24-hour mark for a major storm, are 
19 
greater than historical minor storm levels, and 
20 
can negatively impact SAIFI and CAIDI.   
21 
Q. 
Why does the Company claim that faster 
22 
restoration times impact the annual radial 
23 


--- Page 246 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
245 
 
outage frequency or annual SAIFI? 
1 
A. 
Since these storms do not meet the 24-hour mark 
2 
to qualify as a major storm, associated outages 
3 
are included when calculating the Company’s 
4 
annual average outage frequency performance. 
5 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any concerns with Con 
6 
Edison’s proposed exclusion? 
7 
A. 
Yes.  We have several concerns regarding Con 
8 
Edison’s proposal.   
9 
Q. 
What are the Panel’s main concerns with the 
10 
Company’s proposal? 
11 
A. 
Con Edison has not provided sufficient evidence 
12 
demonstrating how pre-mobilizing 100 or more 
13 
resources impacts the length of restoration 
14 
after an outage event.  Although Con Edison has 
15 
historical data on past storm events, the 
16 
Company only provided a general basis as to why 
17 
it believes that having additional resources on 
18 
site can impact restoration times.  The Company 
19 
did not provide any specific historical data 
20 
detailing any outage events that fell just short 
21 
of lasting 24 hours and qualifying as a major 
22 
storm.  Without appropriate historical 
23 


--- Page 247 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
246 
 
statistics explicitly correlating the 
1 
relationship between retained additional 
2 
resources and outage event durations, we cannot 
3 
adequately assess the Company’s exclusion 
4 
proposal. 
5 
Q. 
What is the Panel’s other concern with the 
6 
Company’s proposal?      
7 
A. 
Tying an exclusion to the Company’s practice of 
8 
pre-mobilizing resources in advance of storm 
9 
events would become problematic in terms of 
10 
justifying when, why and how many resources are 
11 
pre-mobilized for each event. 
12 
Q. 
Please explain. 
13 
A. 
If Con Edison was to exclude every event for 
14 
which the Company secures at least 100 
15 
additional mutual aid storm restoration workers, 
16 
the Company would be able to exclude nearly any 
17 
event from the SAIFI and CAIDI calculations by 
18 
simply securing the minimum 100 workers 
19 
regardless of the severity of the storm event.  
20 
This dynamic could change the Company’s intent 
21 
for securing additional resources because Con 
22 
Edison could be prompted to secure the 100 
23 


--- Page 248 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
247 
 
additional workers for the sole purpose of 
1 
excluding events from the annual threshold 
2 
standard calculations.  For example, if Con 
3 
Edison is close to failing to meet the SAIFI or 
4 
CAIDI targets towards the end of the year, the 
5 
Company may be inclined to over-prepare and 
6 
secure additional resources for every event, 
7 
including minor ones, for the sole purpose of 
8 
excluding the events.  Not only would this be 
9 
circumventing a potential negative revenue 
10 
adjustment for failing to meet a metric, but Con 
11 
Edison would also be improperly using ratepayer 
12 
funds.  As per the Company’s current Major Storm 
13 
Reserve accounting, certain costs associated 
14 
with securing resources in anticipation of an 
15 
event that is forecasted to meet the criteria 
16 
for a major storm but ultimately does not are 
17 
chargeable to the Company’s Major Storm Reserve 
18 
account.  This account is funded through the 
19 
Company’s annual electric revenue requirement 
20 
and collected through rates.  Customers should 
21 
not have to pay for unnecessary storm costs 
22 
incurred for the purpose of excluding outages 
23 


--- Page 249 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
248 
 
from end of year reliability calculations.  
1 
Therefore, pre-mobilization efforts should be 
2 
dictated by storm impact predictions.   
3 
Q. 
Does the Panel have anything further to add 
4 
regarding Con Edison’s proposed exclusion? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  There is already a process available to 
6 
the Company if it believes extraordinary 
7 
circumstances exist that warrant an exemption, 
8 
whereby the utility may petition the Commission 
9 
directly for such an exemption.  The Commission 
10 
affirmed this point in the October 12, 2004 
11 
Order Adopting Changes to Standards on 
12 
Reliability of Electric Service in Case 02-E-
13 
1240, referred to as the Reliability Order, by 
14 
stating that extraordinary circumstances can be 
15 
addressed through a waiver request, and a more 
16 
liberal listing of events that qualify as 
17 
extraordinary would not improve upon 
18 
implementation of the waiver process.  The 
19 
Commission further stated that a case-by-case 
20 
approach to those waivers is more appropriate 
21 
than a listing that might be overly expansive or 
22 
overly constraining.  Additionally, reliability 
23 


--- Page 250 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
249 
 
metrics included in the RPM are intended to 
1 
gauge average performance on an annual basis, 
2 
rather than measure performance over a short 
3 
period of time.  As such, a utility can make up 
4 
for a few days of poor performance over the 
5 
course of a year by the effective management of 
6 
its workforce.  Consequently, reliability 
7 
performance targets are set at a level that 
8 
provides the utility some flexibility while 
9 
still maintaining adequate standards.  It would 
10 
not be appropriate to maintain the current CAIDI 
11 
and SAIFI targets if this exclusion was granted.  
12 
It is for these reasons that we do not support 
13 
the Company’s exclusion proposal. 
14 
Q. 
Does the petition process sufficiently address 
15 
the Company’s concerns? 
16 
A. 
Yes.  Given that there already exists a 
17 
mechanism by which Con Edison can petition the 
18 
Commission for a waiver to exclude specific 
19 
outage events from its year-end reliability 
20 
performance calculations, we find the Company’s 
21 
proposal redundant and unnecessary.  Should Con 
22 
Edison experience an outage event that it 
23 


--- Page 251 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
250 
 
believes to be extraordinary, the Panel 
1 
recommends that the Company use the existing 
2 
process to request an exclusion. 
3 
Q. 
What is the Company’s second proposed change to 
4 
the system-wide threshold standard category of 
5 
the RPM? 
6 
A. 
As stated on page 158 of the EIOP’s initial 
7 
testimony, Con Edison is proposing to replace 
8 
the CAIDI and SAIFI metrics with SAIDI for both 
9 
radial and network systems, inclusive of the 100 
10 
contractor or utility mutual aid worker 
11 
exclusion previously described. 
12 
Q. 
Why does Con Edison propose to replace CAIDI and 
13 
SAIFI with SAIDI? 
14 
A. 
As detailed on pages 158 through 160 of the 
15 
initial testimony of the EIOP, Con Edison 
16 
believes that CAIDI and SAIFI are incomplete 
17 
measurements and provide limited information 
18 
about customer experience.  Con Edison claims, 
19 
on pages 159 and 160 of the EIOP’s initial 
20 
testimony, that CAIDI can be inordinately 
21 
affected by a single interruption, and SAIFI 
22 
does not account for the duration of outages, 
23 


--- Page 252 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
251 
 
but SAIDI, in contrast, measures both frequency 
1 
of interruption and duration. 
2 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with Con Edison’s proposal?  
3 
A. 
No.  We have several concerns regarding the use 
4 
of SAIDI as a replacement for both CAIDI and 
5 
SAIFI. 
6 
Q. 
Please discuss the Panel’s specific concerns. 
7 
A. 
As stated by the Commission in the Reliability 
8 
Order, the frequency of interruptions and the 
9 
duration of interruptions are two separate 
10 
factors that must be measured separately and, 
11 
regardless of SAIDI performance, inadequate 
12 
SAIFI or CAIDI performance must be addressed.  
13 
While SAIDI may measure both frequency of 
14 
interruption and duration, we agree with the 
15 
Commission that CAIDI and SAIFI should be 
16 
measured separately because SAIDI is not truly 
17 
representative of the Company’s reliability 
18 
performance.  Since SAIDI is the total customer 
19 
outage hours over the course of a year, averaged 
20 
over the entire customer base, including those 
21 
customers who are not affected by an 
22 
interruption, SAIDI represents a watered-down 
23 


--- Page 253 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
252 
 
response time that is not reflective of the 
1 
Company’s true outage duration performance.  
2 
This is mostly observed in areas served by a 
3 
network that have low interruption counts.  As 
4 
further discussed in the Reliability Order, 
5 
relying on SAIDI as the sole measure of utility 
6 
performance might potentially mask poor 
7 
performance in frequency or duration.  Since 
8 
SAIDI is essentially the product of SAIFI and 
9 
CAIDI, the Company would be able to overperform 
10 
in one reliability aspect and underperform in 
11 
the other and still appear to have provided 
12 
adequate service throughout the course of a 
13 
year.  Therefore, two distinct measures, one for 
14 
outage frequency and one for outage duration, 
15 
are necessary to best gauge the Company’s 
16 
reliability performance over the course of a 
17 
year.   
18 
Q. 
Are there any other reasons the Panel does not 
19 
support Con Edison’s proposal to replace CAIDI 
20 
and SAIFI with SAIDI? 
21 
A. 
Yes.  Outage frequency and duration are 
22 
independently affected by different factors.  
23 


--- Page 254 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
253 
 
For example, the duration of outages can be 
1 
affected by workforce size and whether an 
2 
interruption occurs during non-working hours. 
3 
These factors have no impact on outage frequency 
4 
as an outage will occur regardless of how many 
5 
Company employees or contractors are onsite or 
6 
working.  Moreover, the use of CAIDI and SAIFI 
7 
is also consistent with the RPMs established by 
8 
the Commission for State’s other electric 
9 
utilities.  
10 
Q. 
Describe the change that Con Edison proposes to 
11 
the program standard category. 
12 
A. 
On page 22 of the Electric Storm Response and 
13 
Resilience Panel’s initial testimony, Con Edison 
14 
proposes to remove the RPM program standard for 
15 
the Non-Network Reliability program in 
16 
Westchester County. 
17 
Q. 
Please further describe this metric and explain 
18 
why Con Edison is proposing it be discontinued.  
19 
A. 
This metric was recommended by Staff and later 
20 
adopted by the Commission in the 2020 Rate Order

ONIM 
21

22 
A. 
The ONIM specifies actions Con Edison must take 
23 


--- Page 263 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
262 
 
to communicate with customers and state, city 
1

6

10

12

13 
information within the required time.  The 
14

15 
for ONIM is $2 million per rate year.

10

A.

12

21 
Q.

22 
and mobilization costs in the current rate plan. 
23 


--- Page 266 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
265 
 
A. 
In the current rate plan, there are two 
1 
deductibles related to pre-staging and 
2 
mobilization costs.  For each event, there is a 
3 
minimum deductible of $500,000 per event.  Costs 
4 
between $500,000 and $2.5 million may be charged 
5 
to the major storm reserve.  For storms in 
6 
excess of $2.5 million, 85 percent of costs is 
7 
charged to the major storm reserve with the 
8 
remaining 15 percent charged to O&M. 
9 
Q. 
What has the Company proposed with regards to 
10 
the treatment of pre-staging and mobilization 
11 
costs? 
12 
A. 
The Company proposes to charge all pre-staging 
13 
costs to the major storm reserve. 
14 
Q. 
What reasons does the Company provide for this 
15 
change? 
16 
A. 
The Company states that more competition for 
17 
resources and more frequent storms have led to 
18 
increased O&M costs.  
19 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the Company’s 
20 
proposal? 
21 
A. 
No.  While it is important that Con Edison take 
22 
the steps necessary to prepare for forecasted 
23 


--- Page 267 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
266 
 
events, these actions must be balanced to avoid 
1 
unnecessary costs being passed onto ratepayers.  
2 
The Panel finds that the existing tiered 
3 
approach to prestaging cost recovery is 
4 
appropriate and encourages good decision-making 
5 
regarding pre-staging, preparation, and 
6 
planning.  Furthermore, the Commission has 
7 
approved similar structures for the utilities in 
8 
every electric rate case in recent years.

23 


--- Page 268 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP

complete planned O&M work that was not completed

2

3

5

7

8

9

10 
A.

14

23 


--- Page 270 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP

patterns and irregularities.  Costs associated

January 1, 2018, and the proposed charges are to 
16 
reflect the Company’s 2021 costs for labor, 
17 
vehicles, corporate overhead, and chemical lab 
18 
testing.  The Company states that the changes in 
19 
costs for these charges is the result of the 
20 
overhead allocation of these tasks. 
21 
Q. 
What did the Panel find in its review? 
22 
A. 
In its response to IR DPS-39-801, included in 
23 


--- Page 275 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
274 
 
Exhibit__(SEIOP-1), the Company explains that 
1 
the main contributing factor to the increasing 
2 
costs is the labor rate, which, in some cases, 
3 
was partially offset by lower engineering and 
4 
administrative and general overheads or lower 
5 
fringe rates.  For example, while the dielectric 
6 
fluid test is impacted by the increase in the 
7 
labor rate, it has been mitigated, in part, by 
8 
the reduction in chemical lab test costs.  Based 
9 
on our review, the Company’s requested changes 
10 
are reasonable.   
11 
Tariff Changes 
12 
Q. 
Is the Panel addressing any specific tariff 
13 
changes proposed in the initial testimony of the 
14 
EIOP? 
15 
A. 
Yes, we will address Con Edison’s proposed 
16 
tariff change related to the Selective 
17 
Underground Program, as discussed on page 163 of 
18 
the EIOP’s initial testimony. 
19 
Q. 
Briefly describe the Company’s proposed tariff 
20 
change. 
21 
A. 
Con Edison is proposing to add a new provision 
22 
stipulating that the Company will bear the cost 
23 


--- Page 276 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
275 
 
of furnishing and installing customer wiring and 
1 
equipment for customers served by the Selective 
2 
Undergrounding Program, provided that the 
3 
customer will maintain the wiring and equipment.  
4 
The Company believes that these costs should be 
5 
socialized and recovered in rates since this 
6 
would advance the Selective Undergrounding 
7 
Program and all customers would benefit when 
8 
service is undergrounded, in the form of quicker 
9 
restoration times and lower storm restoration 
10 
costs. 
11 
Q. 
Does the Panel support the proposed tariff 
12 
change? 
13 
A. 
No.  We disagree with the proposed tariff 
14 
change.  As stated by Con Edison in response to 
15 
IR DPS-13-420, the Company’s estimated cost of 
16 
$10 million per mile used to derive the total 
17 
funding for the Selective Underground Program 
18 
included approximately $12,000 per service 
19 
entrance conversion for modifications to 
20 
customer equipment as needed.  As we stated 
21 
earlier in our testimony, these estimated costs 
22 
are associated with a pilot program initiated as 
23 


--- Page 277 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
276 
 
part of the Joint Outage and Regulatory 
1 
Settlement Agreement in Case 20-E-0422, in which 
2 
Con Edison agreed to pay from shareholder funds 
3 
up to $0.750 million in customer undergrounding 
4 
costs associated with the pilot programs.  Since 
5 
the Company agreed to pay for these costs as 
6 
part of the settlement agreement, it is not 
7 
reasonable that ratepayers should be expected to 
8 
cover these costs going forward, particularly 
9 
considering that the Company has not 
10 
sufficiently illustrated how all customers will 
11 
directly benefit from the undergrounding of 
12 
services for individual.  The Company asserts, 
13 
on page 164 of the EIOP’s initial testimony, 
14 
that the fewer outages that result from a major 
15 
storm, the quicker the Company can restore the 
16 
remaining customers and that, since there will 
17 
be fewer outages, the Company’s overall storm 
18 
restoration costs will be lower.  Yet, in 
19 
response to IR DPS-13-420, Con Edison states 
20 
that the number of long duration outages that 
21 
will be eliminated because of undergrounding 
22 
services, which would lead to the cost 
23 


--- Page 278 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
277 
 
reductions, is difficult to quantify and is 
1 
dependent upon the number of storms that occur 
2 
each year and the area impacted by the storm.  
3 
Given that this program is still in its infancy, 
4 
we do not find it appropriate that all customers 
5 
should pay for the program when the benefits 
6 
that have not yet been fully demonstrated or 
7 
realized.   
8 
Electric Research and Development 
9 
Q. 
Are there any programs under the Company’s 
10 
Research and Development organization that the 
11 
Panel would like to discuss? 
12 
A. 
Yes, we would like to discuss the Medium and 
13 
Heavy Duty Fully Electric Vehicle Development 
14 
program. 
15 
Q. 
In what testimony did the Company present this 
16 
program? 
17 
A. 
This program was proposed in the initial 
18 
testimony of the Shared Services Panel. 
19 
Q. 
Please describe the Medium and Heavy Duty Fully 
20 
Electric Vehicle Development Program. 
21 
A. 
Under this program, the Company is seeking to 
22 
develop a fully electric digger derrick, flush 
23 


--- Page 279 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
278 
 
truck and cable pulling truck with a third-party 
1 
medium- and heavy-duty electric vehicle 
2 
manufacturer.  This would expand on the 
3 
Company’s current development of a fully 
4 
electric material handling bucket truck, which 
5 
was scheduled to be completed in the first 
6 
quarter of 2022. 
7 
Q. 
What O&M funding level is the Company requesting 
8 
for this program? 
9 
A. 
The Company is requesting O&M funding of $1.50 
10 
million, $1.25 million, and $0.75 million in 
11 
RY1, RY2, and RY3, respectively.  
12 
Q. 
What is the Company’s justification for this 
13 
program? 
14 
A. 
As stated on pages 3 and 4 of Exhibit__(SSP-2), 
15 
the Company states that the development of these 
16 
new electric vehicles will help it achieve its 
17 
goals of using alternative fuels in the fleet, 
18 
lower greenhouse gas emissions, reduce petroleum 
19 
usage, and meet the Department of Energy’s 
20 
regulations.  Additionally, this program will 
21 
enable the Company to lead the development and 
22 
tailor the equipment to the electric utility 
23 


--- Page 280 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
279 
 
industry, which would provide Con Edison a 
1 
strategic position and help advance the industry 
2 
forward. 
3 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any recommendations for this 
4 
program? 
5 
A. 
Yes, the Panel recommends rejecting this 
6 
program.  Based on the information provided in 
7 
Exhibit__(SSP-2) and the response to IR DPS-14-
8 
437, included in Exhibit__(SEIOP-1), the costs 
9 
associated with this project do not appear to be 
10 
reasonable for the uncertain benefit it may 
11 
provide customers.  The Company has stated that 
12 
it will not make the decision to begin 
13 
purchasing these electric vehicles until it has 
14 
reviewed the results of the five-year pilot and 
15 
manufacturer availability.  This five-year pilot 
16 
consists of a two-year development period and a 
17 
three-year testing phase wherein the Company 
18 
will cycle the trucks throughout its service 
19 
territory.  Due to the uncertainty of whether 
20 
the Company will purchase additional vehicles 
21 
after the prototypes in this program, Con Edison 
22 
should not move forward with this program until 
23 


--- Page 281 ---
Case 22-E-0064                                            SEIOP 
 
 
 
280 
 
the completion of the testing phase for the 
1 
fully electric material handling bucket truck. 
2 
Q. 
Does this conclude the Panel’s testimony at this 
3 
time? 
4 
A. 
Yes.
